Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Well… I’m not sure if this is the right kind of thing to come in for, but it’s been bothering me more than I’d like to admit. Since I started helping with some safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing at things—like how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I see photos of myself in the newsletter and I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t really see what I’m talking about. I know it probably sounds small, but I can’t seem to stop noticing it.

People say they don’t really see it, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking your appearance in mirrors or taking extra time to make sure you look a certain way, because of these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not all day or anything. I take longer getting ready before those trainings than I used to, and I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when people bring out a camera. And sometimes before a social event, even something I used to look forward to, I’ll hesitate or think maybe I shouldn’t go. It’s embarrassing to admit that, but it has affected things some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. I don’t count it or have a rule like that.\n\nIt’s more… before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face this way and that to see if it looks okay. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your appearance are on your mind for more than an hour a day?\n\nPatient: Probably some days, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting around thinking about myself all day.\n\nIt’s more since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands a lot—like when I’m pointing things out, they look wrinkly or uneven to me. So… yes, I suppose it can add up to more than an hour, especially around those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once you've started noticing something that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that bothered me enough to come in, honestly.\n\nI’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing something out during a safety training, and then it’s like I can’t quite let it go. I keep thinking they look so wrinkly or uneven, even if I know other people may not be paying attention. Since I started helping with some safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing at things—like how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I see photos of myself in the newsletter and I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t really see what I’m talking about. I know it probably sounds small, but I can’t seem to stop noticing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not unbearable or anything, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it does bother me. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I point things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look—and then with the newsletter photos I start thinking my face looks crooked. People say they don’t really see it, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking your appearance in mirrors or taking extra time to make sure you look a certain way, because of these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not all day or anything. Before I teach a class or do one of those senior-center workshops, I might stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways just to see if it looks okay, that sort of thing.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” which feels a bit silly saying out loud. It can make me run a little behind, but I do still get myself there and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Are these concerns about your appearance affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Not in a big dramatic way, I don’t think. People tell me they don’t see it, so I feel a little silly saying it, but once I notice it, it can stick in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean to sit there and think about it on purpose, but it sort of pops in once I’m doing something.\n\nLike when I’m helping with the safety trainings and pointing things out, I suddenly notice my hands and start thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll catch myself looking at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. I know that probably sounds like I’m overthinking it, but it does feel like it comes up on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… no, I don’t think so. I’m still going to work, and I still help with the safety trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI guess it has changed things a little, though. I take longer getting ready before trainings, checking how I look, especially my hands. And I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when I can, like for the newsletter. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much. But I haven’t fully stopped doing them… if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think about your appearance for most of the day, even when you're not getting ready or looking in a mirror?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I’m pointing things out, and then I start thinking my face looks crooked in the newsletter photos even though other people say they don’t see it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not most of the whole day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like once something sets it off, then I have trouble dropping it.

Like, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and then I keep thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And with the newsletter photos, I start staring at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. So it’s not every minute, but it does take up more space in my head than I’d like… if that makes sense.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m not sure if this is the right kind of thing to come in for, but it’s been bothering me more than I’d like to admit. Since I started helping with some safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing at things—like how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I see photos of myself in the newsletter and I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t really see what I’m talking about. I know it probably sounds small, but I can’t seem to stop noticing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not unbearable or anything, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it does bother me. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I point things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look—and then with the newsletter photos I start thinking my face looks crooked. People say they don’t really see it, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking your appearance in mirrors or taking extra time to make sure you look a certain way, because of these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not all day or anything. Before I teach a class or do one of those senior-center workshops, I might stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways just to see if it looks okay, that sort of thing.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” which feels a bit silly saying out loud. It can make me run a little behind, but I do still get myself there and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Are these concerns about your appearance affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Not in a big dramatic way, I don’t think. I’m still going to work, and I still do the volunteering and the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped my life over it.\n\nBut… yes, a little. I take longer getting ready before those trainings than I used to, and I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when people bring out a camera. And sometimes before a social event, even something I used to look forward to, I’ll hesitate or think maybe I shouldn’t go. It’s embarrassing to admit that, but it has affected things some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. I don’t count it or have a rule like that.\n\nIt’s more… before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face this way and that to see if it looks okay. And sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. I know that sounds a little vain, maybe, but it’s just to settle myself. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your appearance are on your mind for more than an hour a day?\n\nPatient: Probably some days, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting around thinking about myself all day.\n\nIt’s more since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands a lot—like when I’m pointing things out, they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then if there’s a newsletter photo, I’ll catch myself thinking my face looks crooked, even when people say they don’t see it. I know that probably sounds like I’m overthinking it, but it does feel like it comes up on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… no, I don’t think so. A little, yes… not so much that I can’t do my job or help with the trainings. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.

But I do notice it slows me down, especially getting ready for trainings, because I’ll check myself more than I need to. And with photos, I’ll be thinking about how to avoid being in them instead of just going along with it. Sometimes before social things, I hesitate more than I used to.

So… yes, it gets in the way some, but I’m still managing. Does that make sense?

It can make me run a little behind, but I do still get myself there and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Are these concerns about your appearance affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Not in a big dramatic way, I don’t think. I’m still going to work, and I still do the volunteering and the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped my life over it.\n\nBut… yes, a little. I take longer getting ready before those trainings than I used to, and I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when people bring out a camera. And sometimes before a social event, even something I used to look forward to, I’ll hesitate or think maybe I shouldn’t go. It’s embarrassing to admit that, but it has affected things some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. I don’t count it or have a rule like that.\n\nIt’s more… before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face this way and that to see if it looks okay. And sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. I know that sounds a little vain, maybe, but it’s just to settle myself. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your appearance are on your mind for more than an hour a day?\n\nPatient: Probably some days, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting around thinking about myself all day.\n\nIt’s more since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands a lot—like when I’m pointing things out, they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then if there’s a newsletter photo, I’ll catch myself thinking my face looks crooked, even when people say they don’t see it. So… yes, I suppose it can add up to more than an hour, especially around those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once you've started noticing something that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that bothered me enough to come in, honestly.\n\nI’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing something out during a safety training, and then it’s like I can’t quite let it go. I keep thinking they look so wrinkly or uneven, even if I know other people may not be paying attention. And then if I see a newsletter photo, I’ll start looking at my face and wondering if it looks crooked. People tell me they don’t see it, so I feel a little silly saying it, but once I notice it, it can stick in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean to sit there and think about it on purpose, but it sort of pops in once I’m doing something.\n\nLike when I’m helping with the safety trainings and pointing things out, I suddenly notice my hands and start thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll catch myself looking at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. I know that probably sounds like I’m overthinking it, but it does feel like it comes up on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… no, I don’t think so. I’m still going to work, and I still help with the safety trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI guess it has changed things a little, though. I take longer getting ready before trainings, checking how I look, especially my hands. And I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when I can, like for the newsletter. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much. But I haven’t fully stopped doing them… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your appearance for most of the day, even when you're not getting ready or looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Not most of the whole day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like once something sets it off, then I have trouble dropping it.\n\nLike, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and then I keep thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And with the newsletter photos, I start staring at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. So it’s not every minute, but it does take up more space in my head than I’d like… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about your appearance are interfering with your ability to focus on other things you're doing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I can’t do my job or help with the trainings. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I do notice it slows me down, especially getting ready for trainings, because I’ll check myself more than I need to. And with photos, I’ll be thinking about how to avoid being in them instead of just going along with it. Sometimes before social things, I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it gets in the way some, but I’m still managing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or participate in social activities because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: A little harder, yes… but I’m still doing my job, and I’m still helping with the trainings. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that I’m slower getting ready for those trainings because I keep checking how I look, and I’ve started trying to avoid photos when they come up. Social things too… I don’t always skip them, but I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make some things harder than they probably need to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I guess… I know some of it is probably my perception, because other people tell me they don’t see what I’m seeing.

But in the moment, it feels real to me. Like when I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I really notice my hands and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, even if everyone says it looks fine.

So I don’t know… I’m trying to believe it’s just me focusing on it too much, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it. Does that answer what you mean?

And sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. I know that sounds a little vain, maybe, but it’s just to settle myself. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your appearance are on your mind for more than an hour a day?\n\nPatient: Probably some days, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting around thinking about myself all day.\n\nIt’s more since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands a lot—like when I’m pointing things out, they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then if there’s a newsletter photo, I’ll catch myself thinking my face looks crooked, even when people say they don’t see it. So… yes, I suppose it can add up to more than an hour, especially around those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once you've started noticing something that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that bothered me enough to come in, honestly.\n\nI’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing something out during a safety training, and then it’s like I can’t quite let it go. I keep thinking they look so wrinkly or uneven, even if I know other people may not be paying attention. And then if I see a newsletter photo, I’ll start looking at my face and wondering if it looks crooked. People tell me they don’t see it, so I feel a little silly saying it, but once I notice it, it can stick in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean to sit there and think about it on purpose, but it sort of pops in once I’m doing something.\n\nLike when I’m helping with the safety trainings and pointing things out, I suddenly notice my hands and start thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll catch myself looking at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. I know that probably sounds like I’m overthinking it, but it does feel like it comes up on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… no, I don’t think so. I’m still going to work, and I still help with the safety trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI guess it has changed things a little, though. I take longer getting ready before trainings, checking how I look, especially my hands. And I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when I can, like for the newsletter. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much. But I haven’t fully stopped doing them… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your appearance for most of the day, even when you're not getting ready or looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Not most of the whole day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like once something sets it off, then I have trouble dropping it.\n\nLike, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and then I keep thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And with the newsletter photos, I start staring at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. So it’s not every minute, but it does take up more space in my head than I’d like… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about your appearance are interfering with your ability to focus on other things you're doing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I can’t do my job or help with the trainings. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I do notice it slows me down, especially getting ready for trainings, because I’ll check myself more than I need to. And with photos, I’ll be thinking about how to avoid being in them instead of just going along with it. Sometimes before social things, I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it gets in the way some, but I’m still managing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or participate in social activities because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: A little harder, yes… but I’m still doing my job, and I’m still helping with the trainings. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that I’m slower getting ready for those trainings because I keep checking how I look, and I’ve started trying to avoid photos when they come up. Social things too… I don’t always skip them, but I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make some things harder than they probably need to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your appearance is actually flawed in the ways you've described, or do you think it's just your own perception that's off?\n\nPatient: I guess… I know some of it is probably my perception, because other people tell me they don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut in the moment, it feels real to me. Like when I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I really notice my hands and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, even if everyone says it looks fine.\n\nSo I don’t know… I’m trying to believe it’s just me focusing on it too much, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes… somewhat. I wouldn’t say it’s ruining my life or anything like that. I’m still working, and I’m still volunteering with the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

But it does affect me day to day in small ways. I take longer getting ready for trainings than I should, because I’m checking how I look. And I’ve started stepping out of photos or trying not to be in them. With social things, I sometimes hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to enjoy without thinking so much.

It’s embarrassing to admit that, but it has affected things some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. I don’t count it or have a rule like that.\n\nIt’s more… before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face this way and that to see if it looks okay. And sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. I know that sounds a little vain, maybe, but it’s just to settle myself. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your appearance are on your mind for more than an hour a day?\n\nPatient: Probably some days, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting around thinking about myself all day.\n\nIt’s more since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands a lot—like when I’m pointing things out, they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then if there’s a newsletter photo, I’ll catch myself thinking my face looks crooked, even when people say they don’t see it. So… yes, I suppose it can add up to more than an hour, especially around those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once you've started noticing something that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that bothered me enough to come in, honestly.\n\nI’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing something out during a safety training, and then it’s like I can’t quite let it go. I keep thinking they look so wrinkly or uneven, even if I know other people may not be paying attention. And then if I see a newsletter photo, I’ll start looking at my face and wondering if it looks crooked. People tell me they don’t see it, so I feel a little silly saying it, but once I notice it, it can stick in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean to sit there and think about it on purpose, but it sort of pops in once I’m doing something.\n\nLike when I’m helping with the safety trainings and pointing things out, I suddenly notice my hands and start thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll catch myself looking at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. I know that probably sounds like I’m overthinking it, but it does feel like it comes up on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… no, I don’t think so. I’m still going to work, and I still help with the safety trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI guess it has changed things a little, though. I take longer getting ready before trainings, checking how I look, especially my hands. And I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when I can, like for the newsletter. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much. But I haven’t fully stopped doing them… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your appearance for most of the day, even when you're not getting ready or looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Not most of the whole day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like once something sets it off, then I have trouble dropping it.\n\nLike, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and then I keep thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And with the newsletter photos, I start staring at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. So it’s not every minute, but it does take up more space in my head than I’d like… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about your appearance are interfering with your ability to focus on other things you're doing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I can’t do my job or help with the trainings. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I do notice it slows me down, especially getting ready for trainings, because I’ll check myself more than I need to. And with photos, I’ll be thinking about how to avoid being in them instead of just going along with it. Sometimes before social things, I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it gets in the way some, but I’m still managing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or participate in social activities because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: A little harder, yes… but I’m still doing my job, and I’m still helping with the trainings. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that I’m slower getting ready for those trainings because I keep checking how I look, and I’ve started trying to avoid photos when they come up. Social things too… I don’t always skip them, but I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make some things harder than they probably need to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your appearance is actually flawed in the ways you've described, or do you think it's just your own perception that's off?\n\nPatient: I guess… I know some of it is probably my perception, because other people tell me they don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut in the moment, it feels real to me. Like when I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I really notice my hands and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, even if everyone says it looks fine.\n\nSo I don’t know… I’m trying to believe it’s just me focusing on it too much, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I wouldn’t say it’s ruining my life or anything like that. I’m still working, and I’m still volunteering with the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it does affect me day to day in small ways. I take longer getting ready for trainings than I should, because I’m checking how I look. And I’ve started stepping out of photos or trying not to be in them. With social things, I sometimes hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affecting me, just not completely stopping me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m not sure if this is the right kind of thing to come in for, but it’s been bothering me more than I’d like to admit. Since I started helping with some safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing at things—like how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I see photos of myself in the newsletter and I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t really see what I’m talking about. I know it probably sounds small, but I can’t seem to stop noticing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not unbearable or anything, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it does bother me. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I point things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look—and then with the newsletter photos I start thinking my face looks crooked. People say they don’t really see it, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking your appearance in mirrors or taking extra time to make sure you look a certain way, because of these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not all day or anything. Before I teach a class or do one of those senior-center workshops, I might stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways just to see if it looks okay, that sort of thing.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” which feels a bit silly saying out loud. It can make me run a little behind, but I do still get myself there and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Are these concerns about your appearance affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Not in a big dramatic way, I don’t think. I’m still going to work, and I still do the volunteering and the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped my life over it.\n\nBut… yes, a little. I take longer getting ready before those trainings than I used to, and I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when people bring out a camera. And sometimes before a social event, even something I used to look forward to, I’ll hesitate or think maybe I shouldn’t go. It’s embarrassing to admit that, but it has affected things some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. I don’t count it or have a rule like that.\n\nIt’s more… before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face this way and that to see if it looks okay. And sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. I know that sounds a little vain, maybe, but it’s just to settle myself. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your appearance are on your mind for more than an hour a day?\n\nPatient: Probably some days, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting around thinking about myself all day.\n\nIt’s more since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands a lot—like when I’m pointing things out, they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then if there’s a newsletter photo, I’ll catch myself thinking my face looks crooked, even when people say they don’t see it. So… yes, I suppose it can add up to more than an hour, especially around those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once you've started noticing something that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that bothered me enough to come in, honestly.\n\nI’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing something out during a safety training, and then it’s like I can’t quite let it go. I keep thinking they look so wrinkly or uneven, even if I know other people may not be paying attention. And then if I see a newsletter photo, I’ll start looking at my face and wondering if it looks crooked. People tell me they don’t see it, so I feel a little silly saying it, but once I notice it, it can stick in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean to sit there and think about it on purpose, but it sort of pops in once I’m doing something.\n\nLike when I’m helping with the safety trainings and pointing things out, I suddenly notice my hands and start thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll catch myself looking at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. I know that probably sounds like I’m overthinking it, but it does feel like it comes up on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… no, I don’t think so. I’m still going to work, and I still help with the safety trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI guess it has changed things a little, though. I take longer getting ready before trainings, checking how I look, especially my hands. And I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when I can, like for the newsletter. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much. But I haven’t fully stopped doing them… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your appearance for most of the day, even when you're not getting ready or looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Not most of the whole day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like once something sets it off, then I have trouble dropping it.\n\nLike, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and then I keep thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And with the newsletter photos, I start staring at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. So it’s not every minute, but it does take up more space in my head than I’d like… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about your appearance are interfering with your ability to focus on other things you're doing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I can’t do my job or help with the trainings. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I do notice it slows me down, especially getting ready for trainings, because I’ll check myself more than I need to. And with photos, I’ll be thinking about how to avoid being in them instead of just going along with it. Sometimes before social things, I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it gets in the way some, but I’m still managing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or participate in social activities because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: A little harder, yes… but I’m still doing my job, and I’m still helping with the trainings. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that I’m slower getting ready for those trainings because I keep checking how I look, and I’ve started trying to avoid photos when they come up. Social things too… I don’t always skip them, but I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make some things harder than they probably need to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your appearance is actually flawed in the ways you've described, or do you think it's just your own perception that's off?\n\nPatient: I guess… I know some of it is probably my perception, because other people tell me they don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut in the moment, it feels real to me. Like when I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I really notice my hands and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, even if everyone says it looks fine.\n\nSo I don’t know… I’m trying to believe it’s just me focusing on it too much, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I wouldn’t say it’s ruining my life or anything like that. I’m still working, and I’m still volunteering with the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it does affect me day to day in small ways. I take longer getting ready for trainings than I should, because I’m checking how I look. And I’ve started stepping out of photos or trying not to be in them. With social things, I sometimes hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affecting me, just not completely stopping me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are unwanted. I mean, I’m not trying to sit there and pick myself apart.\n\nIt’s like since I started helping more with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m noticing my hands — like they look wrinkly or uneven — and then I can’t quite let it go. And with the newsletter photos, I’ll see my face and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little silly, but it does feel like the thoughts just come in on their own. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Is that what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your appearance concerns are more about fixing something you see as flawed or about preventing something from being flawed in the future?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I’m pointing things out, and then I start thinking my face looks crooked in the newsletter photos even though other people say they don’t see it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more about fixing what I’m seeing right now, if that makes sense. Like, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing how wrinkly or uneven my hands look when I’m pointing things out.

And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll start thinking my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t really see it. So… I don’t think I’m as much worried about preventing something later. It’s more that once I notice it, I want it to not look that way. Is that answering it okay?

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m not sure if this is the right kind of thing to come in for, but it’s been bothering me more than I’d like to admit. Since I started helping with some safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing at things—like how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I see photos of myself in the newsletter and I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t really see what I’m talking about. I know it probably sounds small, but I can’t seem to stop noticing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not unbearable or anything, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it does bother me. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I point things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look—and then with the newsletter photos I start thinking my face looks crooked. People say they don’t really see it, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking your appearance in mirrors or taking extra time to make sure you look a certain way, because of these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not all day or anything. Before I teach a class or do one of those senior-center workshops, I might stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways just to see if it looks okay, that sort of thing.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” which feels a bit silly saying out loud. It can make me run a little behind, but I do still get myself there and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Are these concerns about your appearance affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Not in a big dramatic way, I don’t think. I’m still going to work, and I still do the volunteering and the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped my life over it.\n\nBut… yes, a little. I take longer getting ready before those trainings than I used to, and I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when people bring out a camera. And sometimes before a social event, even something I used to look forward to, I’ll hesitate or think maybe I shouldn’t go. It’s embarrassing to admit that, but it has affected things some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. I don’t count it or have a rule like that.\n\nIt’s more… before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face this way and that to see if it looks okay. And sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. I know that sounds a little vain, maybe, but it’s just to settle myself. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your appearance are on your mind for more than an hour a day?\n\nPatient: Probably some days, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting around thinking about myself all day.\n\nIt’s more since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands a lot—like when I’m pointing things out, they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then if there’s a newsletter photo, I’ll catch myself thinking my face looks crooked, even when people say they don’t see it. So… yes, I suppose it can add up to more than an hour, especially around those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once you've started noticing something that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that bothered me enough to come in, honestly.\n\nI’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing something out during a safety training, and then it’s like I can’t quite let it go. I keep thinking they look so wrinkly or uneven, even if I know other people may not be paying attention. And then if I see a newsletter photo, I’ll start looking at my face and wondering if it looks crooked. People tell me they don’t see it, so I feel a little silly saying it, but once I notice it, it can stick in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean to sit there and think about it on purpose, but it sort of pops in once I’m doing something.\n\nLike when I’m helping with the safety trainings and pointing things out, I suddenly notice my hands and start thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll catch myself looking at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. I know that probably sounds like I’m overthinking it, but it does feel like it comes up on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… no, I don’t think so. I’m still going to work, and I still help with the safety trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI guess it has changed things a little, though. I take longer getting ready before trainings, checking how I look, especially my hands. And I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when I can, like for the newsletter. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much. But I haven’t fully stopped doing them… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your appearance for most of the day, even when you're not getting ready or looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Not most of the whole day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like once something sets it off, then I have trouble dropping it.\n\nLike, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and then I keep thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And with the newsletter photos, I start staring at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. So it’s not every minute, but it does take up more space in my head than I’d like… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about your appearance are interfering with your ability to focus on other things you're doing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I can’t do my job or help with the trainings. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I do notice it slows me down, especially getting ready for trainings, because I’ll check myself more than I need to. And with photos, I’ll be thinking about how to avoid being in them instead of just going along with it. Sometimes before social things, I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it gets in the way some, but I’m still managing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or participate in social activities because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: A little harder, yes… but I’m still doing my job, and I’m still helping with the trainings. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that I’m slower getting ready for those trainings because I keep checking how I look, and I’ve started trying to avoid photos when they come up. Social things too… I don’t always skip them, but I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make some things harder than they probably need to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your appearance is actually flawed in the ways you've described, or do you think it's just your own perception that's off?\n\nPatient: I guess… I know some of it is probably my perception, because other people tell me they don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut in the moment, it feels real to me. Like when I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I really notice my hands and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, even if everyone says it looks fine.\n\nSo I don’t know… I’m trying to believe it’s just me focusing on it too much, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I wouldn’t say it’s ruining my life or anything like that. I’m still working, and I’m still volunteering with the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it does affect me day to day in small ways. I take longer getting ready for trainings than I should, because I’m checking how I look. And I’ve started stepping out of photos or trying not to be in them. With social things, I sometimes hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affecting me, just not completely stopping me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are unwanted. I mean, I’m not trying to sit there and pick myself apart.\n\nIt’s like since I started helping more with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m noticing my hands — like they look wrinkly or uneven — and then I can’t quite let it go. And with the newsletter photos, I’ll see my face and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little silly, but it does feel like the thoughts just come in on their own. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance concerns are more about fixing something you see as flawed or about preventing something from being flawed in the future?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about fixing what I’m seeing right now, if that makes sense. Like, I came in because since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing how wrinkly or uneven my hands look when I’m pointing things out.\n\nAnd then with the newsletter photos, I’ll start thinking my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t really see it. So… I don’t think I’m as much worried about preventing something later. It’s more that once I notice it, I want it to not look that way. Is that answering it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So… I don’t think I’m as much worried about preventing something later. It’s more that once I notice it, I want it to not look that way.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m not sure if this is the right kind of thing to come in for, but it’s been bothering me more than I’d like to admit. Since I started helping with some safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing at things—like how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I see photos of myself in the newsletter and I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t really see what I’m talking about. I know it probably sounds small, but I can’t seem to stop noticing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not unbearable or anything, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But it does bother me. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I point things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look—and then with the newsletter photos I start thinking my face looks crooked. People say they don’t really see it, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it, but it still gets under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking your appearance in mirrors or taking extra time to make sure you look a certain way, because of these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not all day or anything. Before I teach a class or do one of those senior-center workshops, I might stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I’ll put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways just to see if it looks okay, that sort of thing.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” which feels a bit silly saying out loud. It can make me run a little behind, but I do still get myself there and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Are these concerns about your appearance affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Not in a big dramatic way, I don’t think. I’m still going to work, and I still do the volunteering and the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped my life over it.\n\nBut… yes, a little. I take longer getting ready before those trainings than I used to, and I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when people bring out a camera. And sometimes before a social event, even something I used to look forward to, I’ll hesitate or think maybe I shouldn’t go. It’s embarrassing to admit that, but it has affected things some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. I don’t count it or have a rule like that.\n\nIt’s more… before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face this way and that to see if it looks okay. And sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. I know that sounds a little vain, maybe, but it’s just to settle myself. It can make me run a little behind, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about your appearance are on your mind for more than an hour a day?\n\nPatient: Probably some days, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting around thinking about myself all day.\n\nIt’s more since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands a lot—like when I’m pointing things out, they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then if there’s a newsletter photo, I’ll catch myself thinking my face looks crooked, even when people say they don’t see it. So… yes, I suppose it can add up to more than an hour, especially around those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once you've started noticing something that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the part that bothered me enough to come in, honestly.\n\nI’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing something out during a safety training, and then it’s like I can’t quite let it go. I keep thinking they look so wrinkly or uneven, even if I know other people may not be paying attention. And then if I see a newsletter photo, I’ll start looking at my face and wondering if it looks crooked. People tell me they don’t see it, so I feel a little silly saying it, but once I notice it, it can stick in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean to sit there and think about it on purpose, but it sort of pops in once I’m doing something.\n\nLike when I’m helping with the safety trainings and pointing things out, I suddenly notice my hands and start thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll catch myself looking at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. I know that probably sounds like I’m overthinking it, but it does feel like it comes up on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… no, I don’t think so. I’m still going to work, and I still help with the safety trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI guess it has changed things a little, though. I take longer getting ready before trainings, checking how I look, especially my hands. And I’ve started trying to duck out of photos when I can, like for the newsletter. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much. But I haven’t fully stopped doing them… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your appearance for most of the day, even when you're not getting ready or looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Not most of the whole day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like once something sets it off, then I have trouble dropping it.\n\nLike, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and then I keep thinking they look wrinkly or uneven. And with the newsletter photos, I start staring at my face and thinking it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. So it’s not every minute, but it does take up more space in my head than I’d like… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about your appearance are interfering with your ability to focus on other things you're doing?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I can’t do my job or help with the trainings. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I do notice it slows me down, especially getting ready for trainings, because I’ll check myself more than I need to. And with photos, I’ll be thinking about how to avoid being in them instead of just going along with it. Sometimes before social things, I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it gets in the way some, but I’m still managing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or participate in social activities because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: A little harder, yes… but I’m still doing my job, and I’m still helping with the trainings. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that I’m slower getting ready for those trainings because I keep checking how I look, and I’ve started trying to avoid photos when they come up. Social things too… I don’t always skip them, but I hesitate more than I used to.\n\nSo it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make some things harder than they probably need to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your appearance is actually flawed in the ways you've described, or do you think it's just your own perception that's off?\n\nPatient: I guess… I know some of it is probably my perception, because other people tell me they don’t see what I’m seeing.\n\nBut in the moment, it feels real to me. Like when I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I really notice my hands and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I’ll look at my face and think it looks crooked, even if everyone says it looks fine.\n\nSo I don’t know… I’m trying to believe it’s just me focusing on it too much, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I wouldn’t say it’s ruining my life or anything like that. I’m still working, and I’m still volunteering with the trainings, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it does affect me day to day in small ways. I take longer getting ready for trainings than I should, because I’m checking how I look. And I’ve started stepping out of photos or trying not to be in them. With social things, I sometimes hesitate before going, even if it’s something I used to enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affecting me, just not completely stopping me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are unwanted and come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are unwanted. I mean, I’m not trying to sit there and pick myself apart.\n\nIt’s like since I started helping more with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m noticing my hands — like they look wrinkly or uneven — and then I can’t quite let it go. And with the newsletter photos, I’ll see my face and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little silly, but it does feel like the thoughts just come in on their own. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance concerns are more about fixing something you see as flawed or about preventing something from being flawed in the future?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about fixing what I’m seeing right now, if that makes sense. Is that answering it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are a part of you and something you would miss if they were gone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d miss them, no. They don’t feel like some important part of me or anything like that.\n\nI mean… I’m used to being a little self-conscious, I guess, but these particular thoughts aren’t something I want to keep. If they were gone, I think I’d probably feel relieved. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I guess… yes, probably more distress than actual disruption, if I’m being fair.
